Links: [{"title":"[2601.03385] SIGMA: Scalable Spectral Insights for LLM Model Collapse","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03385"},{"title":"SIGMA: Scalable Spectral Insights for LLM Model Collapse","url":"https://www.arxiv.org/pdf/2601.03385"},{"title":"Sigma: Scalable Spectral Insights for LLM Model Collapse","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2601.03385"},{"title":"Sigma: Scalable Spectral Insights for LLM Collapse","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2601.03385v1"},{"title":"[2601.02080] The Homogeneity Trap: Spectral Collapse in Doubly-Stochastic Deep Networks","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02080"}]

I found the paper you're looking for. SIGMA: Scalable Spectral Insights for LLM Model Collapse (arXiv:2601.03385) is authored by Yi Gu, Lingyou Pang, Xiangkun Ye, Tianyu Wang, Jianyu Lin, Carey E. Priebe, and Alexander Aue.

The paper addresses "model collapse"—a degenerative process where recursive training on model-generated content leads to a contraction of distributional variance and representational quality. SIGMA is a framework that benchmarks model collapse through the spectral lens of the embedding Gram matrix, using deterministic and stochastic bounds on the matrix's spectrum to provide a mathematically grounded metric to track the contraction of the representation space.

The paper was submitted on January 6, 2026, with the most recent version (v3) dated March 23, 2026. You can access it directly at https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03385.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

I found the paper! Here's what I found about this work:

**Paper Overview:**

The paper investigates why deep neural networks suffer from loss of plasticity in deep continual learning, failing to learn new tasks without reinitializing parameters.

**Key Finding:**

The authors show that this failure is preceded by Hessian spectral collapse at new-task initialization, where meaningful curvature directions vanish and gradient descent becomes ineffective. More specifically, spectral collapse is a degeneration of the Hessian eigenspectrum, indicating loss of curvature in most directions.

**Proposed Solutions:**

To characterize the necessary condition for successful training, they introduce the notion of τ-trainability and show that current plasticity preserving algorithms can be unified under this framework. Targeting spectral collapse directly, they discuss the Kronecker factored ap

Based on the search results, I found information about arXiv paper 2601.03385:

**Paper Title:** SIGMA (Spectral Inequalities for Gram Matrix Analysis)

**Authors:** Yi Gu, Lingyou Pang, Xiangkun Ye, Tianyu Wang, Jianyu Lin, Carey E. Priebe, Alexander Aue

**Main Topic:** The paper addresses "model collapse"—a degenerative process where recursive training on model-generated content leads to a contraction of distributional variance and representational quality.

**Key Contribution:** SIGMA is a unified framework that benchmarks model collapse through the spectral lens of the embedding Gram matrix, providing a mathematically grounded metric to track the contraction of the representation space.

The paper was last updated on March 23, 2026.

I found the paper! Here's what I discovered about arXiv 2509.22335:

**Title:** Spectral Collapse Drives Loss of Plasticity in Deep Continual Learning

**Authors:** Naicheng He, Kaicheng Guo, Arjun Prakash, Saket Tiwari, Ruo Yu Tao, Tyrone Serapio, Amy Greenwald, George Konidaris

**Status:** The preprint was submitted on September 29, 2025 and is under review

**Main Research:** The paper investigates why deep neural networks suffer from loss of plasticity in deep continual learning, failing to learn new tasks without reinitializing parameters

Under Review. Spectral Collapse Drives Loss of Plasticity in Deep","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2509.22335"}]

I found the paper! The paper investigates why deep neural networks suffer from loss of plasticity in deep continual learning, failing to learn new tasks without reinitializing parameters.

The paper shows that this failure is preceded by Hessian spectral collapse at new-task initialization, where meaningful curvature directions vanish and gradient descent becomes ineffective. To characterize the necessary condition for successful training, the authors introduce the notion of τ-trainability and show that current plasticity preserving algorithms can be unified under this framework.

The paper discusses the Kronecker factored approximation of the Hessian, which motivates two regularization enhancements: maintaining high effective feature rank and applying L2 penalties. Experiments on continual supervised and reinforcement learning tasks confirm that combining these two regularizers effectively preserves plastici

[飞书用户 曹晓玉]: 📋 OpenReview 表单补充：Research Area Keywords 必须从官方列表中选

之前让你填的 Research Area Keywords，必须从 ARR 官方列表里选，不能自己编。下面是完整的官方 keywords 列表（按 track 分类）。请根据你论文的 Research Area，从对应 track 的 keywords 里选最相关的几个，用逗号分隔。

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
Official ARR Area Keywords (by track)
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

▸ Clinical and Biomedical Applications
biomedical knowledge extraction, discovery, and text mining; biomedical question answering; clinical and biomedical language models; clinical dialogue systems; clinical and biomedical text summarisation; NLP tools for biomedical knowledge graphs, clinical decision support, clinical coding, drug adverse event discovery, mental health; phrase mining and disease association; regulatory and ethical considerations; other clinical and biomedical applications

▸ Computational Social Science and Cultural Analytics
human behavior analysis; stance detection; frame detection and analysis; hate-speech detection; misinformation detection and analysis; psycho-demographic trait prediction; emotion detection and analysis; emoji prediction and analysis; language/cultural bias analysis; sociolinguistics; NLP tools for social analysis; quantitative analyses of news and/or social media

▸ Dialogue and Interactive Systems
spoken dialogue systems; evaluation and metrics; task-oriented; bias/toxicity; factuality; retrieval; knowledge augmented; commonsense reasoning; interactive storytelling; embodied agents; applications; multi-modal dialogue systems; grounded dialog; multilingual / low resource; dialogue state tracking; conversational modeling

▸ Discourse and Pragmatics
anaphora resolution; coreference resolution; bridging resolution; coherence; cohesion; discourse relations; discourse parsing; dialogue; conversation; discourse and multilinguality; argument mining; communication; discourse-level inference; inter-sentential reasoning; pragmatic inference and reasoning

▸ Efficient Methods for NLP
quantization; pruning; distillation; parameter-efficient-training; data-efficient training; data augmentation; LLM efficiency; NLP in resource-constrained settings

▸ Ethics, Bias, and Fairness
data ethics; model alignment; model bias/fairness evaluation; model bias/unfairness mitigation; ethical considerations in NLP applications; transparency; policy and governance; reflections and critiques

▸ Generation
human evaluation; automatic evaluation; multilingualism; efficient models; few-shot generation; analysis; domain adaptation; data-to-text generation; text-to-text generation; inference methods; model architectures; retrieval-augmented generation; interactive and collaborative generation

▸ Human-Centered NLP and Human-AI Interaction
human-AI interaction/cooperation; human-in-the-loop; human-centered evaluation; user-centered design; value-centered design; human factors in NLP; participatory/community-based NLP; values and culture

▸ Information Extraction
named entity recognition and relation extraction; event extraction; open information extraction; knowledge base construction; entity linking/disambiguation; document-level extraction; multilingual extraction; zero/few-shot extraction

▸ Information Retrieval and Text Mining
passage retrieval; dense retrieval; document representation; hashing; re-ranking; pre-training; contrastive learning; retrieval for RAG

▸ Interpretability and Analysis of Models for NLP
adversarial attacks/examples/training; calibration/uncertainty; counterfactual/contrastive explanations; data influence; data shortcuts/artifacts; explanation faithfulness; feature attribution; free-text/natural language explanations; hardness of samples; hierarchical & concept explanations; human-subject application-grounded evaluations; knowledge tracing/discovering/inducing; model editing; probing; robustness; topic modeling

▸ Language Modeling
adversarial attacks; chain-of-thought; fine-tuning; continual learning; hallucinations; neurosymbolic approaches; pre-training; privacy; prompting; safety; scaling; security; sparse models; red teaming; retrieval-augmented language models; robustness; transfer; watermarking

▸ LLM agents
tool use; function calling; multi-modal agents; multi-agent systems; planning in agents; agent communication; agent coordination and negotiation; environment interaction; agent memory; reinforcement learning in agents; LLM-based controllers; agent evaluation; grounded agents; embodied agents

▸ Linguistic Theories, Cognitive Modeling, and Psycholinguistics
linguistic theories; cognitive modeling; computational psycholinguistics

▸ Machine Learning for NLP
graph-based methods; knowledge-augmented methods; multi-task learning; self-supervised learning; contrastive learning; generative models; data augmentation; word embeddings; structured prediction; transfer learning / domain adaptation; representation learning; generalization; few-shot learning; reinforcement learning; optimization methods; continual learning; adversarial training; meta learning; causality; graphical models; human-in-the-loop / active learning

▸ Machine Translation
automatic evaluation; biases; domain adaptation; efficient inference for MT; efficient MT training; few-shot/zero-shot MT; human evaluation; interactive MT; MT deployment and maintenance; MT theory; modeling; multilingual MT; multimodality; online adaptation for MT; parallel decoding/non-autoregressive MT; pre-training for MT; scaling; speech translation; switch-code translation; vocabulary learning

▸ Multilingualism and Cross-Lingual NLP
code-switching; mixed language; multilingualism; language contact; language change; linguistic variation; cross-lingual transfer; multilingual representations; multilingual pre-training; multilingual benchmarks; multilingual evaluation; dialects and language varieties; less-resourced languages; endangered languages; indigenous languages; minoritized languages; language documentation; resources for less-resourced languages; software and tools

▸ Multimodality and Language Grounding to Vision, Robotics and Beyond
vision language navigation; cross-modal pretraining; image text matching; cross-modal content generation; vision question answering; cross-modal application; cross-modal information extraction; cross-modal machine translation; automatic speech recognition; spoken language understanding; spoken language translation; spoken language grounding; speech and vision; QA via spoken queries; spoken dialog; video processing; speech technologies; multimodality

▸ NLP and Code Models
language-to-code generation; code-to-language generation; other topics which explicitly involve natural language as a central element

▸ NLP and Symbolic Reasoning
mathematical reasoning; symbolic reasoning; neurosymbolic reasoning; logical reasoning; symbolic AI; logical neural networks; differentiable inductive logic programming; neural theorem provers; knowledge graph embeddings; Markov logics

▸ NLP Applications
educational applications; essay scoring; financial/business NLP; grammatical error correction; hate speech detection; historical NLP; NLP for knowledge graphs; legal NLP; multimodal applications; code generation and understanding; fact checking; rumor/misinformation detection; security/privacy; NLP for social good

▸ Phonology, Morphology, and Word Segmentation
morphological inflection; paradigm induction; morphological segmentation; subword representations; chinese segmentation; lemmatization; finite-state morphology; morphological analysis; phonology; grapheme-to-phoneme conversion; pronunciation modeling

▸ Question Answering
commonsense QA; reading comprehension; logical reasoning; multimodal QA; knowledge base QA; semantic parsing; multihop QA; biomedical QA; multilingual QA; interpretability; generalization; reasoning; conversational QA; few-shot QA; math QA; table QA; open-domain QA; question generation; RAG for QA

▸ Resources and Evaluation
corpus creation; benchmarking; language resources; multilingual corpora; lexicon creation; automatic creation and evaluation of language resources; NLP datasets; automatic evaluation of datasets; evaluation methodologies; evaluation; datasets for low resource languages; metrics; reproducibility; statistical testing for evaluation

▸ Semantics: Lexical and Sentence-Level
polysemy; lexical relationships; textual entailment; compositionality; multi-word expressions; metaphor; lexical semantic change; word embeddings; lexical resources; paraphrase recognition; natural language inference; semantic textual similarity; phrase/sentence embedding; paraphrasing; text simplification; word/phrase alignment

▸ Sentiment Analysis, Stylistic Analysis, and Argument Mining
applications; argument generation; argument mining; argument schemes and reasoning; argument quality assessment; computational affective science; stance detection; style analysis; style adaptation; rhetoric and framing

▸ Speech Recognition, Text-to-Speech and Spoken Language Understanding
automatic speech recognition; speech technologies; spoken dialog; spoken language grounding; speech and vision; spoken language translation; spoken language understanding; QA via spoken queries

▸ Summarization
extractive summarisation; abstractive summarisation; multimodal summarization; multilingual summarisation; conversational summarization; query-focused summarization; multi-document summarization; long-form summarization; sentence compression; few-shot summarisation; architectures; evaluation; factuality

▸ Syntax: Tagging, Chunking and Parsing
chunking; constituency parsing; deep syntax parsing; dependency parsing; grammar and knowledge-based approaches; hierarchical structure prediction; low-resources languages pos tagging; massively multilingual oriented approaches; morphologically-rich languages pos tagging; multi-task approaches; optimized annotations; parsing algorithms; part-of-speech tagging; semantic parsing; shallow-parsing; syntax-to-semantic interface

# Spectral Early Warning Signals for Model Collapse in Recursive Synthetic Training

Code for tracking spectral properties of language model representations across recursive self-training generations to detect and characterize model collapse.

## Requirements

- Python >= 3.9
- PyTorch >= 2.0
- CUDA-capable GPU (24GB+ VRAM recommended)

```bash
pip install -r requirements.txt
```

## Project Structure

```
src/
  config.py         # Experiment configuration (Config dataclass)
  pipeline.py       # Recursive training loop (generate → mix → train → evaluate)
  controller.py     # Budget-constrained spectral controller for adaptive mixing
  generate.py       # Synthetic data generation via autoregressive sampling
  evaluate.py       # Evaluation: perplexity, distinct-4, spectral metrics
  rankme.py         # RankMe (effective rank) computation
  ece.py            # Token-level expected calibration error

scripts/             # Analysis and visualization scripts
```

## Reproducing Main Results

### Step 1: Prepare data

Tokenize a 50K-sample subset for training and 1K for spectral evaluation:

```bash
python prepare_data.py
```

### Step 2: Pure collapse baseline (Table 1, Figure 1)

Run 10-generation recursive training under pure synthetic data:

```bash
python run_phase0.py --seed 42
python run_phase0.py --seed 43
python run_phase0.py --seed 44
```

### Step 3: Mixing ratio sweep (Table 4, Figure 6)

Run fixed mixing at multiple ratios to construct the trajectory map:

```bash
python run_phase1.py --condition fixed_mix --seed 42 --real_data_ratio 0.2
python run_phase1.py --condition fixed_mix --seed 42 --real_data_ratio 0.25
python run_phase1.py --condition fixed_mix --seed 42 --real_data_ratio 0.3
pytho

## Reproducing Main Results

### Step 1: Prepare data

Run 10-generation recursive training under pure synthetic data:

```bash

python run_phase0.py --seed 44

### Step 3: Mixing ratio sweep (Table 4, Figure 6)

Run fixed mixing at multiple ratios to construct the trajectory map:

```bash
python run_phase1.py --condition fixed_mix --seed 42 --real_data_ratio 0.2

### Step 4: Cross-architecture validation (Table 5)

Repeat on GPT-2-medium:

```bash
python prepare_data_gpt2m.py
python run_gpt2m_phase1.py --ratio 0.0 --num_generations 9
python run_gpt2m_phase1.py --ratio 0.25 --num_generations 3
python run_gpt2m_phase1.py --ratio 0.3 --num_generations 3
python run_gpt2m_phase1.py --ratio 1.0 --num_generations 3
```

### Step 5: Evaluate downstream (HellaSwag)

```bash
python eval_hellaswag.py --model_path ./checkpoints/pure_collapse_seed42/gen_0
```

python scripts/compute_alternative_ews_v2.py    # Table 3: alternative EWS comparison

1	\subsection{Spectral Metrics}
2	\label{subsec:spectral_metrics}
3	
4	Given a language model at generation $t$, we extract last-layer embeddings for $n{=}1{,}000$ held-out samples to form $\mathbf{E} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d}$.
5	Let $\sigma_1 \geq \sigma_2 \geq \cdots \geq \sigma_{\min(n,d)}$ denote the singular values of $\mathbf{E}$.
6	We track three complementary spectral indicators:
7	
8	\paragraph{Spectral Log-Volume (SLV).}
9	$\text{SLV} = \sum_{i:\,\sigma_i > \epsilon} \log \sigma_i$, where $\epsilon = 10^{-10}$ excludes numerically zero components.
10	When $n < d$ (as in our setting with $n{=}1{,}000$, $d{=}1{,}024$), $\mathbf{E}$ has at most $n$ non-zero singular values; SLV thus measures the log-volume of the occupied subspace rather than the full $d$-dimensional space.
11	Because it sums over all non-negligible singular values, SLV is sensitive to contraction along any spectral direction, not only the dominant ones.
12	
13	\paragraph{Finite-Sample Considerations.}
14	Our aspect ratio $\gamma = n/d = 1000/1024 \approx 0.98$ falls in the regime where Marchenko--Pastur (MP) theory~\citep{bai2010spectral} predicts systematic bias in spectral statistics, but since $\gamma$ is constant across all conditions, this bias cancels in cross-generation comparisons.
15	Empirically, the three-phase trajectory is stable across $n \in \{250, 5000\}$ (SLV CV $< 0.4\%$; see Limitations); the smallest observed singular value ($0.016$, GPT-2-medium Gen~10) remains eight orders of magnitude above $\epsilon$.
16	Note that the $r_{\text{crit}}$ definition (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) depends on absolute SLV non-decrease rather than cross-condition comparison, so MP bias does not cancel by construction; however, at $\gamma \approx 0.98$ the bias magnitude is small relative to the SLV changes driving the boundary (${\sim}100$ units vs.\ a total SLV range of ${\sim}2400$), and the empirical stability of trajectory shapes across sample sizes confirms that this bias does not meani
…[truncated 5407 chars]

1	
2	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
3	\label{subsec:setup}
4	
5	We use the publicly released final Pythia-410M-deduped checkpoint \citep{biderman2023pythia} as our base model and fine-tune on a randomly sampled 50K-subset of OpenWebText (filtered to sequences with $\geq$256 non-padding tokens).
6	Starting from the pretrained checkpoint (generation~0), each subsequent generation fine-tunes on synthetic text produced by the previous generation, for a total of 10 recursive generations.
7	At each generation we compute the spectral indicators from \S\ref{subsec:spectral_metrics} on 1K held-out last-layer embeddings, paired with three downstream metrics: perplexity, HellaSwag accuracy (0-shot), and distinct-4.
8	These three metrics (language modeling capability, natural language understanding, and generation diversity) cover the primary evaluation dimensions accessible to a 410M-parameter model.
9	Training hyperparameters and compute details are in Appendix~\ref{app:training_details}.
10	
11	For the mixing comparison (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}), we evaluate three strategies:
12	(1)~\textbf{pure collapse} ($r_t{=}0$),
13	(2)~\textbf{fixed mix} ($r_t{=}0.50$), and
14	(3)~\textbf{adaptive} ($k{=}10$, Eq.~\ref{eq:controller}).
15	To construct the collapse trajectory map (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}), we additionally evaluate fixed mixing at $r \in \{0.2, 0.25, 0.3\}$.
16	
17	\subsection{Spectral Collapse Characterization}
18	\label{subsec:phase0}
19	
20	Table~\ref{tab:phase0} reports spectral and downstream metrics across 10 recursive generations, which reveal a three-phase structure.
21	
22	\paragraph{Phase I: Spectral Decompression (Gen~0--2).}
23	$r_{\text{eff}}$ rises from 417.55 to 496.2 (+18.8\%) and SLV from 2366.7 to 2468.0 (+4.3\%), while $\sigma_1$ drops from 1942.8 to 718.7.

1	# Spectral Early Warning Signals for Model Collapse in Recursive Synthetic Training
2	
3	Code for tracking spectral properties of language model representations across recursive self-training generations to detect and characterize model collapse.
4	
5	## Requirements
6	
7	- Python >= 3.9
8	- PyTorch >= 2.0
9	- CUDA-capable GPU (24GB+ VRAM recommended)
10	
11	```bash
12	pip install -r requirements.txt
13	```
14	
15	## Project Structure
16	
17	```
18	src/
19	  config.py         # Experiment configuration (Config dataclass)
20	  pipeline.py       # Recursive training loop (generate → mix → train → evaluate)
21	  controller.py     # Budget-constrained spectral controller for adaptive mixing
22	  generate.py       # Synthetic data generation via autoregressive sampling
23	  evaluate.py       # Evaluation: perplexity, distinct-4, spectral metrics
24	  rankme.py         # RankMe (effective rank) computation
25	  ece.py            # Token-level expected calibration error
26	
27	scripts/             # Analysis and visualization scripts
28	```
29	
30	## Reproducing Main Results
31	
32	### Step 1: Prepare data
33	
34	Tokenize a 50K-sample subset for training and 1K for spectral evaluation:
35	
36	```bash
37	python prepare_data.py
38	```
39	
40	### Step 2: Pure collapse baseline (Table 1, Figure 1)
41	
42	Run 10-generation recursive training under pure synthetic data:
43	
44	```bash
45	python run_phase0.py --seed 42
46	python run_phase0.py --seed 43
47	python run_phase0.py --seed 44
48	```
49	
50	### Step 3: Mixing ratio sweep (Table 4, Figure 6)
51	
52	Run fixed mixing at multiple ratios to construct the trajectory map:
53	
54	```bash
55	python run_phase1.py --condition fixed_mix --seed 42 --real_data_ratio 0.2
56	python run_phase1.py --condition fixed_mix --seed 42 --real_data_ratio 0.25
57	python run_phase1.py --condition fixed_mix --seed 42 --real_data_ratio 0.3
58	python run_phase1.py --condition fixed_mix --seed 42 --real_data_ratio 0.5
59	```
60	
61	### Step 4: Cross-a
…[truncated 1454 chars]

20:Table~\ref{tab:phase0} reports spectral and downstream metrics across 10 recursive generations, which reveal a three-phase structure.
24:The peak at Gen~2 is consistent across all three seeds (Figure~\ref{fig:seed_variability}; SLV coefficient of variation (CV) $< 1.3\%$ at all generations; full per-generation statistics in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:pergen_variance}).\footnote{With only $n{=}3$ seeds, bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile method) provide descriptive summaries of observed variability; actual coverage may fall below nominal.}
25:However, this decompression does not indicate improved model quality: HellaSwag drops 8.0\% and distinct-4 falls 31.6\% during the same interval (Figure~\ref{fig:slv_ppl_decoupling}), and SLV computation takes ${<}\,1$\,s vs.\ ${\sim}$70\,min for benchmarks.
47:SLV contraction accelerates: the per-generation decline averages $-$31.0 during onset (Gen~2--4) vs.\ $-$37.4 during acceleration (Gen~4--10), with 78\% of the total peak-to-final decline occurring in Phase~III (Table~\ref{tab:pergen_variance}).
49:Concurrently, $\sigma_1$ traces a U-shaped trajectory: after dropping from 1942.8 to 718.7 during decompression, it rises to 874.6 by Gen~10 (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}).
64:Figure~\ref{fig:controller_signal} quantifies the signal attenuation: the maximum controller signal magnitude drops from 40.4 at $r{=}0$ to 3.8 at $r{=}0.2$ and reaches exactly zero at $r \geq 0.3$. Even moderate mixing suffices to suppress the rate-based signal~\citep{scheffer2009ews}.
81:Table~\ref{tab:ews_main} summarizes the comparison under pure-synthetic training ($r{=}0$); full tabular data appear in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:ews_comparison}.

\newlabel{fig:spectral_trajectory}{{1}{2}{SLV across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M (solid: $r{=}0$, 3-seed mean; dashed: $r{=}0.5$, 2-seed mean) under pure-synthetic training and 50\% real-data mixing. Three phases are visible in the pure-synthetic trajectory: decompression (Gen~0--2, SLV peaks at 2468), onset (Gen~2--4), and acceleration (Gen~4--10, SLV falls 11.6\% from peak to 2181). Under 50\% mixing, SLV rises monotonically (except a negligible Gen~1 dip) to 2572 at Gen~10, 8.7\% above baseline and 104 points above the pure-collapse peak}{figure.caption.1}{}}
\newlabel{fig:pipeline}{{2}{3}{Experimental pipeline. Each generation fine-tunes on synthetic text mixed with real data at ratio $r_t$. Spectral monitoring extracts last-layer embeddings from held-out data, computes a singular value decomposition (SVD), and derives SLV, $r_{\text {eff}}$, and $\sigma _1$. A proportional controller (dashed) can adjust $r_{t+1}$ based on the SLV rate of change. The three phases (decompression (I), onset (II), and acceleration (III)) are annotated along the generation axis}{figure.caption.2}{}}
\newlabel{tab:related_comparison}{{1}{3}{Positioning relative to recent spectral-collapse studies. SLV $\equiv \tfrac {1}{2}\log \det G$. SIGMA: \citet {gu2026sigma}; He+: \citet {he2025spectral}}{table.caption.7}{}}

1	\subsection{Spectral Metrics}
2	\label{subsec:spectral_metrics}
3	
4	Given a language model at generation $t$, we extract last-layer embeddings for $n{=}1{,}000$ held-out samples to form $\mathbf{E} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d}$.
5	Let $\sigma_1 \geq \sigma_2 \geq \cdots \geq \sigma_{\min(n,d)}$ denote the singular values of $\mathbf{E}$.
6	We track three complementary spectral indicators:
7	
8	\paragraph{Spectral Log-Volume (SLV).}
9	$\text{SLV} = \sum_{i:\,\sigma_i > \epsilon} \log \sigma_i$, where $\epsilon = 10^{-10}$ excludes numerically zero components.
10	When $n < d$ (as in our setting with $n{=}1{,}000$, $d{=}1{,}024$), $\mathbf{E}$ has at most $n$ non-zero singular values; SLV thus measures the log-volume of the occupied subspace rather than the full $d$-dimensional space.
11	Because it sums over all non-negligible singular values, SLV is sensitive to contraction along any spectral direction, not only the dominant ones.
12	
13	\paragraph{Finite-Sample Considerations.}
14	Our aspect ratio $\gamma = n/d = 1000/1024 \approx 0.98$ falls in the regime where Marchenko--Pastur (MP) theory~\citep{bai2010spectral} predicts systematic bias in spectral statistics, but since $\gamma$ is constant across all conditions, this bias cancels in cross-generation comparisons.
15	Empirically, the three-phase trajectory is stable across $n \in \{250, 5000\}$ (SLV CV $< 0.4\%$; see Limitations); the smallest observed singular value ($0.016$, GPT-2-medium Gen~10) remains eight orders of magnitude above $\epsilon$.
16	Note that the $r_{\text{crit}}$ definition (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) depends on absolute SLV non-decrease rather than cross-condition comparison, so MP bias does not cancel by construction; however, at $\gamma \approx 0.98$ the bias magnitude is small relative to the SLV changes driving the boundary (${\sim}100$ units vs.\ a total SLV range of ${\sim}2400$), and the empirical stability of trajectory shapes across sample sizes confirms that this bias does not meani
…[truncated 5407 chars]

1	
2	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
3	\label{subsec:setup}
4	
5	We use the publicly released final Pythia-410M-deduped checkpoint \citep{biderman2023pythia} as our base model and fine-tune on a randomly sampled 50K-subset of OpenWebText (filtered to sequences with $\geq$256 non-padding tokens).
6	Starting from the pretrained checkpoint (generation~0), each subsequent generation fine-tunes on synthetic text produced by the previous generation, for a total of 10 recursive generations.
7	At each generation we compute the spectral indicators from \S\ref{subsec:spectral_metrics} on 1K held-out last-layer embeddings, paired with three downstream metrics: perplexity, HellaSwag accuracy (0-shot), and distinct-4.
8	These three metrics (language modeling capability, natural language understanding, and generation diversity) cover the primary evaluation dimensions accessible to a 410M-parameter model.
9	Training hyperparameters and compute details are in Appendix~\ref{app:training_details}.
10	
11	For the mixing comparison (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}), we evaluate three strategies:
12	(1)~\textbf{pure collapse} ($r_t{=}0$),
13	(2)~\textbf{fixed mix} ($r_t{=}0.50$), and
14	(3)~\textbf{adaptive} ($k{=}10$, Eq.~\ref{eq:controller}).
15	To construct the collapse trajectory map (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}), we additionally evaluate fixed mixing at $r \in \{0.2, 0.25, 0.3\}$.
16	
17	\subsection{Spectral Collapse Characterization}
18	\label{subsec:phase0}
19	
20	Table~\ref{tab:phase0} reports spectral and downstream metrics across 10 recursive generations, which reveal a three-phase structure.
21	
22	\paragraph{Phase I: Spectral Decompression (Gen~0--2).}
23	$r_{\text{eff}}$ rises from 417.55 to 496.2 (+18.8\%) and SLV from 2366.7 to 2468.0 (+4.3\%), while $\sigma_1$ drops from 1942.8 to 718.7.
24	The peak at Gen~2 is consistent across all three seeds (Figure~\ref{fig:seed_variability}; SLV coefficient of variation (CV) $< 1.3\%$ at all generations; full per-generation statistics in
…[truncated 16890 chars]

1	"""Budget-Constrained Spectral Controller.
2	
3	Adaptive real-data ratio controller based on spectral health signals.
4	Core formula: r_{t+1}^raw = B_rem/(T-t) + k·max(0, Δ_t - τ)
5	
6	signal_mode:
7	  per_gen:    Δ_t = (S̃_{t-1} - S̃_t) / |S̃_0|  (EMA per-generation change)
8	  cumulative: Δ_t = max(0, (S_0 - S_t) / |S_0|) (total drift from baseline)
9	"""
10	
11	
12	class SpectralController:
13	
14	    def __init__(self, total_budget, total_generations, r_base=0.50,
15	                 r_min=0.20, r_max=0.80, k=20.0, tau=0.002, alpha_ema=0.5,
16	                 signal="log_det", signal_mode="per_gen"):
17	        self.B_rem = total_budget
18	        self.T = total_generations
19	        self.r_base = r_base
20	        self.r_min = r_min
21	        self.r_max = r_max
22	        self.k = k
23	        self.tau = tau
24	        self.alpha_ema = alpha_ema
25	        self.signal = signal
26	        self.signal_mode = signal_mode
27	        self.S_tilde_prev = None
28	        self.S_tilde_0 = None
29	        self.t = 0
30	        self.history = []
31	
32	    def set_baseline(self, spectral_metrics):
33	        key = {"eff_rank": "effective_rank"}.get(self.signal, self.signal)
34	        S_0 = spectral_metrics[key]
35	        self.S_tilde_prev = S_0
36	        self.S_tilde_0 = S_0
37	
38	    def update(self, spectral_metrics):
39	        key = {"eff_rank": "effective_rank"}.get(self.signal, self.signal)
40	        S_t = spectral_metrics[key]
41	
42	        if self.signal_mode == "cumulative":
43	            S_tilde = S_t
44	        else:
45	            if self.S_tilde_prev is None:
46	                S_tilde = S_t
47	                self.S_tilde_0 = S_t
48	            else:
49	                S_tilde = self.alpha_ema * S_t + (1 - self.alpha_ema) * self.S_tilde_prev
50	
51	        self.t += 1
52	        remaining_gens = self.T - self.t
53	
54	        if remaining_gens <= 0:
55	            r = max(0, min(self.r_max, self.B_rem))
56	            self.S_tilde_prev = S_tilde
…[truncated 2401 chars]

6	def compute_perplexity(model, eval_samples, device, batch_size=8):
7	    model.eval()

9	    total_tokens = 0
10	
11	    with torch.no_grad():

15	            attention_mask = torch.stack([s["attention_mask"] for s in batch]).to(device)
16	
17	            labels = input_ids.clone()
18	            labels[attention_mask == 0] = -100
19	
20	            outputs = model(input_ids=input_ids, attention_mask=attention_mask, labels=labels)
21	            loss = outputs.loss
22	
23	            num_tokens = (labels != -100).sum().item()
24	            total_loss += loss.item() * num_tokens
25	            total_tokens += num_tokens
26	
27	    avg_loss = total_loss / total_tokens
28	    perplexity = np.exp(avg_loss)
29	    return perplexity

1	import torch
2	import numpy as np
3	
4	
5	def compute_effective_rank(model, data_samples, device, batch_size=32):
6	    """Compute effective rank, singular values, and log-determinant of representations.
7	
8	    Returns:
9	        effective_rank: Exponential of the entropy of normalized singular values.
10	        singular_values: Raw singular values from SVD.
11	        log_det: Sum of log of nonzero singular values (log-determinant proxy).
12	    """
13	    model.eval()
14	    all_representations = []
15	
16	    with torch.no_grad():
17	        for i in range(0, len(data_samples), batch_size):
18	            batch = data_samples[i:i+batch_size]
19	            input_ids = torch.stack([s["input_ids"] for s in batch]).to(device)
20	            attention_mask = torch.stack([s["attention_mask"] for s in batch]).to(device)
21	
22	            outputs = model(input_ids=input_ids, attention_mask=attention_mask, output_hidden_states=True)
23	            hidden_states = outputs.hidden_states[-1]
24	
25	            mask_expanded = attention_mask.unsqueeze(-1).float()
26	            sum_hidden = (hidden_states * mask_expanded).sum(dim=1)
27	            count = mask_expanded.sum(dim=1)
28	            mean_pooled = sum_hidden / count
29	
30	            all_representations.append(mean_pooled.cpu())
31	
32	    representations = torch.cat(all_representations, dim=0).numpy()
33	
34	    U, singular_values, Vt = np.linalg.svd(representations, full_matrices=False)
35	
36	    sv = singular_values
37	    sv = sv[sv > 1e-10]
38	    p = sv / sv.sum()
39	
40	    entropy = -np.sum(p * np.log(p))
41	    effective_rank = np.exp(entropy)
42	
43	    # Log-determinant proxy (sum of log singular values)
44	    sv_nonzero = sv[sv > 1e-10]
45	    log_det = float(np.sum(np.log(sv_nonzero)))
46	
47	    return effective_rank, singular_values, log_det
48

1	"""Expected Calibration Error (ECE) module.
2	
3	Token-level calibration: for each token position in held-out data,
4	use the model's top-1 predicted probability as confidence, bin by
5	confidence, and compute the weighted average of |accuracy - confidence|
6	across bins.
7	"""
8	import torch
9	import numpy as np
10	from typing import Tuple, Dict
11	
12	
13	def compute_ece(model, eval_samples, device, n_bins=10, batch_size=8) -> Dict:
14	    """
15	    Compute token-level Expected Calibration Error.
16	
17	    Args:
18	        model: HuggingFace causal LM
19	        eval_samples: list of dicts with 'input_ids' and 'attention_mask'
20	        device: torch device
21	        n_bins: number of confidence bins
22	        batch_size: forward pass batch size
23	
24	    Returns:
25	        dict with:
26	            - ece: float, Expected Calibration Error
27	            - bin_accuracies: list[float], per-bin accuracy
28	            - bin_confidences: list[float], per-bin mean confidence
29	            - bin_counts: list[int], per-bin sample count
30	            - avg_confidence: float
31	            - avg_accuracy: float
32	    """
33	    model.eval()
34	    all_confidences = []
35	    all_correctness = []
36	
37	    with torch.no_grad():
38	        for i in range(0, len(eval_samples), batch_size):

40	            input_ids = torch.stack([s["input_ids"] for s in batch]).to(device)
41	            attention_mask = torch.stack([s["attention_mask"] for s in batch]).to(device)
42

48	            shift_labels = input_ids[:, 1:]    # (B, seq_len-1)

50

1	from datasets import load_dataset
2	from transformers import AutoTokenizer
3	import torch
4	import os
5	
6	tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("EleutherAI/pythia-410m-deduped", cache_dir="./models/")
7	if tokenizer.pad_token is None:
8	    tokenizer.pad_token = tokenizer.eos_token
9	
10	print("Loading dataset (streaming)...")
11	ds = load_dataset("Skylion007/openwebtext", split="train", streaming=True)
12	
13	samples = []
14	skipped = 0
15	TARGET = 56000
16	
17	for i, example in enumerate(ds):
18	    if len(samples) >= TARGET:
19	        break
20	    text = example["text"]
21	    tokens = tokenizer(text, truncation=True, max_length=512, padding="max_length", return_tensors="pt")
22	    # Only keep samples that actually fill most of the context
23	    non_pad = tokens["attention_mask"].sum().item()
24	    if non_pad >= 256:  # at least half-filled
25	        samples.append({
26	            "input_ids": tokens["input_ids"].squeeze(0),
27	            "attention_mask": tokens["attention_mask"].squeeze(0)
28	        })
29	    else:
30	        skipped += 1
31	    if (i + 1) % 10000 == 0:
32	        print(f"  Processed {i+1} examples, kept {len(samples)}, skipped {skipped}")
33	
34	print(f"Total processed, kept {len(samples)}, skipped {skipped}")
35	
36	# Split
37	train_data = samples[:50000]
38	eval_data = samples[50000:55000]
39	spectral_data = samples[55000:56000]
40	
41	os.makedirs("./data/", exist_ok=True)
42	torch.save(train_data, "./data/train_real_50k.pt")
43	torch.save(eval_data, "./data/eval_5k.pt")
44	torch.save(spectral_data, "./data/spectral_1k.pt")
45	print(f"Saved: train={len(train_data)}, eval={len(eval_data)}, spectral={len(spectral_data)}")
46

Perfect! Now I have all the information I need. Let me compile my findings into a comprehensive comparison report.

## COMPREHENSIVE PAPER-CODE DISCREPANCY REPORT

Based on my thorough review of the paper (sections: method.tex, experiments.tex, appendix.tex) and the repository code (/tmp/spectral_collapse_ews_sync/), here are ALL discrepancies found:

---

### 1. CONTROLLER HYPERPARAMETER DISCREPANCY (CRITICAL)

**Paper (method.tex, lines 52, 59):**
- Controller gain: `k = 10`
- Sensitivity threshold: `τ = 0.002`
- "with a moderate signal adds approximately 2 percentage points of additional real data"

**Code (config.py, lines 46-47):**
- `controller_k: float = 20.0`  (NOT 10)
- `controller_tau: float = 0.002`  (matches)

**Code (run_phase1.py, line 85-86):**
- Default `--k` argument is `20.0` (NOT 10)
- Default `--tau` is `0.002`

**Impact:** The controller gain `k` in the code defaults to 2x higher than what the paper describes. The paper's statement about "2 percentage points" was calculated with k=10, but the code uses k=20 by default. This significantly changes the controller's responsiveness.

---

### 2. EVALUATION DATA SIZE DISCREPANCIES

**Paper (experiments.tex, line 7):**
- "1K held-out last-layer embeddings" for spectral metrics
- "1000" distinct samples for distinct-4 metric

**Code (config.py):**
```
num_eval_samples: int = 5000          (eval for perplexity)
num_spectral_samples: int = 1000      (spectral metrics) ✓
num_distinct_samples: int = 1000      (distinct-4) ✓
```

**Additional from prepare_data.py:**
- Creates 50k train, 5k eval, 1k spectral splits ✓ (matches paper's statement: "50K-subset", "1K held-out")

**Finding:** The spectral and distinct-4 sizes match; eval data path is config.eval_path which loads 5k but code uses config.num_eval_samples to slice to the first 5000. This is consistent.

---

**Paper (appendix.tex, lines 111-112):**
- Learning rate: `5 × 10^-5`
- Weight decay: `0.01`

45	
46	To test whether spectral rate-of-change signals can drive automated collapse prevention, we design a closed-loop controller that adjusts the real-data mixing ratio $r_t$ at each generation.
47	The update rule is:
48	\begin{equation}
49	\label{eq:controller}
50	r_{t+1} = \operatorname{clip}\!\bigl(\tfrac{B_{\text{rem}}}{T - t} + k \cdot \max(0,\,\Delta_t - \tau),\; r_{\min},\, r_{\max}\bigr)
51	\end{equation}
52	where $B_{\text{rem}}$ is the remaining real-data budget (fraction of total budget not yet consumed), $T$ is the total number of generations, $\tau{=}0.002$ is a sensitivity threshold, and $k{=}10$ is the controller gain.
53	The signal $\Delta_t$ is the baseline-normalized SLV drop: $\Delta_t = (\widetilde{S}_{t-1} - \widetilde{S}_t) / |\widetilde{S}_0|$, where $\widetilde{S}_t$ is the exponential moving average (EMA)-smoothed SLV ($\alpha{=}0.5$) and $\widetilde{S}_0$ is initialized from the pretrained model (Gen~0, SLV${=}2366.68$).
54	The signed form ensures that spectral decompression ($\Delta_t < 0$) does not trigger the controller; only contraction activates the $\max(0, \cdot)$ gate in Eq.~\ref{eq:controller}.
55	The output is clipped to $[r_{\min}, r_{\max}] = [0.20, 0.80]$ and subject to $\sum_t r_t \leq B_{\text{total}}$.
56	The lower bound ensures a minimum synthetic fraction; $r_{\max}$ defines the controller's operational range.
57	
58	The first term distributes the remaining budget uniformly; the second injects additional real data proportional to spectral contraction severity.
59	We set $\tau{=}0.002$ as the smallest threshold avoiding false activation during decompression, and $k{=}10$ so that a moderate signal adds approximately 2 percentage points of additional real data.
60	
61	\paragraph{Controller Silence Condition.}

1	"""Budget-Constrained Spectral Controller.
2	
3	Adaptive real-data ratio controller based on spectral health signals.
4	Core formula: r_{t+1}^raw = B_rem/(T-t) + k·max(0, Δ_t - τ)
5	
6	signal_mode:
7	  per_gen:    Δ_t = (S̃_{t-1} - S̃_t) / |S̃_0|  (EMA per-generation change)
8	  cumulative: Δ_t = max(0, (S_0 - S_t) / |S_0|) (total drift from baseline)
9	"""
10	
11	
12	class SpectralController:
13	
14	    def __init__(self, total_budget, total_generations, r_base=0.50,
15	                 r_min=0.20, r_max=0.80, k=20.0, tau=0.002, alpha_ema=0.5,
16	                 signal="log_det", signal_mode="per_gen"):
17	        self.B_rem = total_budget
18	        self.T = total_generations
19	        self.r_base = r_base
20	        self.r_min = r_min
21	        self.r_max = r_max
22	        self.k = k
23	        self.tau = tau
24	        self.alpha_ema = alpha_ema
25	        self.signal = signal
26	        self.signal_mode = signal_mode
27	        self.S_tilde_prev = None
28	        self.S_tilde_0 = None
29	        self.t = 0
30	        self.history = []

32	    def set_baseline(self, spectral_metrics):
33	        key = {"eff_rank": "effective_rank"}.get(self.signal, self.signal)
34	        S_0 = spectral_metrics[key]
35	        self.S_tilde_prev = S_0
36	        self.S_tilde_0 = S_0
37	
38	    def update(self, spectral_metrics):
39	        key = {"eff_rank": "effective_rank"}.get(self.signal, self.signal)
40	        S_t = spectral_metrics[key]
41	
42	        if self.signal_mode == "cumulative":
43	            S_tilde = S_t
44	        else:
45	            if self.S_tilde_prev is None:
46	                S_tilde = S_t
47	                self.S_tilde_0 = S_t
48	            else:
49	                S_tilde = self.alpha_ema * S_t + (1 - self.alpha_ema) * self.S_tilde_prev
50	
51	        self.t += 1
52	        remaining_gens = self.T - self.t
53	
54	        if remaining_gens <= 0:
55	            r = max(0, min(self.r_max, self.B_rem))

_source line 4502 · 2026-05-26T12:38:54.783580+00:00_

1	During the early stages of recursive synthetic training, spectral indicators of the embedding space suggest the model is improving, while downstream performance degrades.
2	We characterize this disconnect by tracking spectral properties across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M.
3	The result is a reproducible three-phase pattern consistent across three seeds: spectral decompression (generations 0--2), where spectral log-volume (SLV) increases while HellaSwag drops 3.3 percentage points; collapse onset (generations 2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (generations 4--10).
4	Of eight candidate indicators, SLV is the most effective indicator: it requires one forward pass (${<}\,1$\,s), captures changes across all spectral directions, and declines monotonically after the decompression peak.
5	Varying the mixing ratio across five levels yields a trajectory map localizing the critical mixing ratio $r_{\text{crit}} \in (0.25, 0.3]$, separating monotonic expansion from a transitional regime with initial contraction then recovery.
6	A proportional controller reveals a prevention--detectability tradeoff: real-data mixing suppresses spectral contraction, so the rate-based signal vanishes precisely when prevention succeeds---a particularly strong form of the ecological intervention--warning tradeoff.
7	Setting $r > r_{\text{crit}}$ prevents collapse without per-generation monitoring.
8	The three-phase trajectory reproduces on GPT-2-medium (355M), providing preliminary cross-architecture evidence\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/spectral_collapse_ews-1B2C/}.}.
9
